Every Tuesday morning, I sit down at the dining room table—the mahogany one we bought at Sears Roebuck when we first got married, I still use a lace tablecloth on it to prevent scratches—and I get out my checkbook. The one with the little Thomas Kinkade paintings on the checks. I write out the checks for the electric bill, the water bill, the newspaper subscription, and I balance the ledger down to the very last penny. \n\nA person who isn't worth much wouldn't be able to keep a household running so efficiently, would they? I still make sure the trash cans are out by the curb on Wednesday nights, and I still remember to water the Boston fern in the sunroom. So, no, my self-worth is perfectly intact. I don't feel guilty, and I don't feel worthless. It's just... like I said before, it's just this heavy blankness. I know I did my best, and I know I'm still a capable woman, but knowing that doesn't make the house feel any less empty. But to answer your question, no, no guilt at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been moving more slowly than usual, or that it takes you longer to get things done, like buttoning your blouse or walking across the room?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Oh, heavens no, I wouldn't say that. I've always had a very brisk step, doctor. Even when I was a young woman, people used to tell me I walked like I had somewhere important to be, and that hasn't changed, not one bit. \n\nYou mentioned buttoning a blouse... well, just this morning, I put on my favorite soft blue one. It’s the one with the little pearl buttons down the front—not the cheap plastic ones, but real mother-of-pearl that catches the light so beautifully if you stand near the bedroom window. I buttoned it right up in no time at all. My fingers are just as nimble as they ever were. I don't fumble with the buttonholes, and I certainly don't take any longer to get dressed than the fifteen minutes I've allotted myself every morning since 1962. \n\nAnd walking across the room? No, no, I'm not shuffling or dragging my feet. We have this beautiful runner rug in the main hallway—it's an oriental pattern, lots of deep reds and navy blues, which we bought at an estate sale over in Westbridge about thirty years ago to protect the hardwood floors—and I walk down that hallway at the exact same pace I always have. I know precisely how long it takes me to get from the bedroom to the kitchen to put the kettle on. I walk right past the grandfather clock my husband used to wind every Sunday, and I don't take a second longer to cross the house than I used to. \n\nI even walked all the way down the driveway to the mailbox yesterday afternoon. It's a fairly long driveway, paved with asphalt that gets terribly hot in the summer, though it's quite pleasant right now, and I marched right down there, retrieved the mail, and walked right back up. Most of it was just catalogs and a flyer for the new supermarket, nothing important, but I didn't need to stop and rest or catch my breath. \n\nSo no, my body isn't slowing down. I can move just as quickly and efficiently as I ever did. It's just... well, like I told you earlier, even though my legs work perfectly fine and I can walk just as fast, it just feels like there's nowhere that really matters to walk to anymore. But my physical speed? Oh, that's perfectly normal. I haven't slowed down a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about harming yourself or not wanting to live anymore?\n\nPatient: Harming myself? Oh, my goodness, doctor, no. I could never do something like that. I've always believed life is a precious thing, even when it gets difficult. I certainly haven't had any thoughts about not wanting to be here. \n\nWhy, just this morning, I was sitting at the kitchen island—it has that lovely butcher block top that my husband oiled every single month without fail. He always used food-grade mineral oil, never olive oil, because olive oil can go rancid over time and ruin the wood, you know. Anyway, I was sitting right there on the wooden stool, taking my daily vitamins. I have one of those little plastic pill organizers, the ones with the days of the week printed in bold black letters on the lids. Mine is a cheerful bright pink color. My son David bought it for me at the pharmacy on Main Street about three years ago because I kept forgetting if I'd taken my calcium supplement. \n\nEvery single morning, I pop open the little lid for the day, and I take my calcium pill, a vitamin D, and a fish oil capsule, and I swallow them down with a small glass of orange juice. The pulp-free kind, of course, because I just can't stand the texture of pulp in my drinks. Now, if I didn't want to live, I certainly wouldn't be going through all that trouble every single day to keep my bones strong and my heart healthy, would I? \n\nI've never once thought about doing anything to hurt myself. It's a terrible thing to even consider. I know some people get to that dark place when something awful happens, but that's just not me. Even with this... this heavy, quiet feeling in the house, and that blankness I mentioned before, I still get up and I still take care of myself. I still make sure I'm eating my three meals, I still lock the doors at night, and I still look both ways before I cross the street to get to the community center. I'm going to keep right on living, just exactly like I'm supposed to. So no, doctor, absolutely no thoughts like that at all.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Unusually high or overly energetic? Oh, heavens, no. I'm not that sort of person at all, doctor. I've always been very even-keeled. My husband used to say I was as predictable as the sunrise, and he meant it as a compliment, of course. I don't get overly worked up or go flying around the house with too much energy, and I certainly don't have racing thoughts. My mind is usually very organized. 

And as for not needing much sleep... well, I've always valued a good night's rest. Every single night, for as long as we've lived in this house, I start getting ready for bed at exactly nine-thirty. I go into the master bathroom—the one with the peach-colored tiles we had put in back in 1978, they were very popular then, you know, though I suppose they look a bit dated now—and I wash my face with cold cream. The Pond's cold cream, the classic one with the light green lid. Then I brush my teeth and I'm in bed by ten o'clock sharp. I have a very specific mattress, a firm Sealy Posturepedic, because a soft mattress is just terrible for your lumbar spine, and I sleep right through until six-thirty in the morning. I've never been one of those people who can just stay up all night or run around on two hours of sleep. Goodness, I'd be absolutely exhausted! I simply must have my eight hours.

And irritable? No, no, I wouldn't say so. I try to be pleasant to everyone, I really do. Even when the neighbor's boy throws the morning paper right into my azalea bushes instead of onto the front porch—which he's done three times this month, mind you, and those bushes are very delicate, especially the pink ones—I just put on my slippers and go out and retrieve it myself. I don't fly off the handle or get terribly irritated with him. He's just a boy on a bicycle, after all. It's just not in my nature to be snappy or irritable. 

So, no, I've never had a time like that where I was just bursting with energy or my mind was going a mile a minute. I've always been very steady, very calm. Just... well, just a bit quieter lately, I suppose. But no periods of being unusually high or anything like that.

Usually, just seeing the sun hit the yard in the mornings... oh, it used to fill me with such warmth, you know? Just a quiet, happy feeling that made waking up worthwhile. \n\nBut lately... well, that's why I finally agreed to come in. I came because even when my children come over and sit with me on the porch, or when that beautiful sun is right on my garden, I just... I feel completely flat inside. It's the strangest, most awful thing. It's like the part of me that could be glad, or even just comforted by my own family being there with me, it just completely shut off. And I keep waiting for it to come back, I keep sitting out there thinking today will be the day I smile and actually feel it in my chest, but it's just this heavy blankness. I don't know how else to explain it, but it's like the joy has just been scooped right out of me, no matter how hard they try to cheer me up or how lovely the day is.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take pleasure in things you used to enjoy, like working in your garden or spending time with your family?\n\nPatient: Yes, exactly. That is exactly what I was just trying to say, doctor. It's... well, it's just exactly that. I mean, my daughter, she brought her little ones over just this past Sunday, and they were running around the hydrangeas—those are the big blue ones I planted back in, oh, it must have been '98 or '99—and usually, oh, usually I would be out there with them, or at least sitting on the porch with a glass of iced tea, just soaking it all in and feeling so blessed. \n\nBut yes, like I said, even with them right there on the porch with me, even with the sun shining right down on the garden just the way I have always loved... I just feel completely flat inside. It is the most frightening thing. I sit there in my favorite wicker chair, the one my late husband bought me for our anniversary, and I watch them, and I know in my head that I love them, but the part of me that could actually be glad they are there? Or the part that feels comforted by my own family being close? It really has just completely shut off. It is like someone flipped a switch in my chest and left me in the dark. I try, I really do try to force a smile so they don't worry, but they know. My daughter knows. Because I just can't feel the good things anymore, no matter how beautiful the day is or how much I want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel down or sad most days, even when there’s no particular reason for it?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... it's not exactly sadness. That's the thing that's so hard to explain to my daughter, because she keeps asking me, \"Mom, are you depressed? Are you crying?\" And I tell her, no, I'm not crying. I haven't shed a single tear, which is strange for me, because I've always been a bit of a weeper, you know? A sad commercial on the television, or a sweet card from the grandchildren, and I'd be reaching for a tissue. But now? No. \n\nIt's not that heavy, weeping kind of sadness that you might expect. It's more like... well, like I was saying before, it's just this blankness. I don't wake up feeling like there's a dark cloud over my head, making me want to sob into my pillow. I wake up, and I look at the clock—it's usually around 6:15, my body just naturally wakes up then after all these years—and I just feel empty. Not sad, just... nothing. I go about my morning, I make my toast, I pour my decaf coffee, and I just sit there at the kitchen table, looking out the window, and I don't feel down, exactly. I just don't feel anything at all. So, no, I wouldn't say I feel sad or down most days. It's more of an absence of feeling, really, rather than a sad feeling.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you feel a depressed mood most days, even when there isn't a specific reason for it?\n\nPatient: Well, no, doctor, I... I feel like we might be going around in circles a bit here, because it's just like I was trying to explain a moment ago. It's not a depressed mood, not in the way you're asking. When I think of being depressed, I think of my sister, Mary—she passed away back in 2012, God rest her soul—but when she was going through her divorce in the late seventies, oh, she was depressed. She couldn't get out of bed, she cried all the time, she wore her bathrobe until three in the afternoon. That's a depressed mood to me. \n\nBut for me, right now, it's not that at all. I get up, I get dressed. I put on a nice blouse, I fix my hair, even if I'm not going anywhere but the front porch. I don't feel that heavy, sinking, tearful kind of sadness. I'm not sitting around weeping for no reason. It's just... well, it's that emptiness I mentioned. It's like my heart is just a hollow room right now. There's no sadness in it, but there's no happiness either. It's just quiet. Too quiet. So, no, I really wouldn't call it a depressed mood. I wouldn't use that word at all. I just feel completely blank.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in all or almost all activities, including those you used to enjoy, like tending to your garden or being with your family?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I... forgive me, but I have to ask if you're looking at the right page in your notes there. Because, well, unless my hearing aids are acting up again—and I just put fresh batteries in them this morning, the little size 312 ones, they always give me such a fuss to get into the little door with my arthritis—I could swear you just asked me this exact same thing. About the garden and the family. \n\nI mean, I really don't know what else I can tell you that I haven't already said just a moment ago. Yes, I've lost interest. Just like I was explaining with the porch and the wicker chair and my grandchildren. I haven't even gone out to check on my tomato plants, and usually, I'm out there with my sun hat and my little green watering can every single morning before the sun gets too hot. But now, I just look out the kitchen window at them and I don't feel the slightest urge to go out there. \n\nIt's all just exactly what I said before. That blankness. I really don't have anything new to add about it, doctor. I don't mean to be difficult, I really don't, but we seem to be going over the exact same ground here, and I've already told you everything about how I'm just not enjoying my family or my yard the way I used to. Is there... is there some other part of it you're trying to figure out? Because I just don't have anything else to say about the gardening.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the emptiness and loss of interest in your usual activities?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... it's been about two weeks now. Yes, exactly two weeks this past Tuesday. I remember because Tuesday is usually the day the trash gets picked up, and I was just standing there at the kitchen counter, trying to decide if I should take the bin out myself or wait for my neighbor, Mr. Henderson, to offer—he's such a nice man, always looks out for me—when it happened. \n\nThat was the day my husband... well, that was the day he collapsed. Right there in our kitchen. We've had that same linoleum floor since 1998, a sort of pale yellow pattern, and he just went down on it so suddenly. One minute he was pouring himself a glass of orange juice, and the next, he was just on the floor. I called 911, and the paramedics came. It felt like it took them hours, but I know it was only a few minutes. They rushed in with all their equipment, pushing past my little hall table and knocking over a picture frame—not that I cared about the frame, of course, it was just a picture of the dog we had in the nineties—and they worked on him right there on the floor. \n\nThey were shouting things, numbers and medical terms I didn't understand, and the radio on their shoulders was crackling, and I just stood there, backed into the corner by the refrigerator, watching them pump his chest. It was so loud, doctor, and so chaotic. And ever since that morning, ever since they finally got him on the stretcher and took him out the front door... that's when this emptiness started. Two weeks of just... nothing. Just sitting in that same kitchen, looking at the spot on the floor, and feeling completely blank. So, yes, it's been about two weeks since all of this started.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really don't think so, doctor. I mean, my weight has always been pretty steady, ever since I had my youngest, David, back in '68. I've always hovered right around the same mark on the scale. We have this old scale in the bathroom, one of the ones with the little dial that spins around, not those new digital things with the red numbers. It belonged to my mother, actually, and it's got a little rust spot near the left footpad, but it still works just fine. And I haven't noticed the dial moving any differently than it usually does. \n\nAs for my appetite... well, I suppose I'm eating about the same as I always have. I'm not really paying much attention to it, to be honest. I still make my toast in the morning—two slices of wheat bread, toasted just enough to be warm but not too crunchy, with a little scrape of butter. And I still have my cup of tea. But I'm just eating because it's time to eat, you know? It's like I'm going through the motions. \n\nI'll sit down at the kitchen table—right near where he fell, but I try not to look at that spot—and I'll chew my toast, and I don't really taste it. It's just cardboard in my mouth. But I finish it, because that's what you do at breakfast time. And for dinner, I might heat up a can of soup. Chicken noodle, usually, the kind with the little star-shaped pasta. My husband always liked the chunky kind, but I prefer the stars. But even the soup, it's just... it's just warm liquid. I'm not hungry for it, but I'm not actively avoiding it either. I just eat it because the clock says it's six o'clock. So, no, I wouldn't say my appetite has changed, not really. I'm still eating my regular meals. It's just that the food doesn't mean anything anymore. It's just another blank thing in a blank day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately, like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up earlier than usual and not being able to go back to sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, sleep... sleep is about the same, I suppose. I mean, I'm not really having any new trouble with it. I've always been a bit of a light sleeper, you know? Ever since I was a girl, my mother used to say a mouse sneezing three houses down could wake me up. And then when my husband and I bought this house back in '72, we got the bedroom right over the street, and for years I'd wake up every time the milkman came by. \n\nSo I still go to bed around the same time, right after the ten o'clock news finishes. I always watch the local news, channel 4, because I like the weatherman, the one with the bright ties. And then I go upstairs, and I do my little routine—wash my face with that cold cream I've used for fifty years, brush my teeth, put on my nightgown. The blue cotton one with the little embroidered flowers around the collar. \n\nAnd I get into bed, and... well, I fall asleep eventually. I might lie there for a bit, staring at the ceiling fan—we put that fan in during the heatwave of '88, and it's got this one blade that wobbles just a tiny bit if you put it on the highest speed—but I don't think it takes me any longer to drift off than it usually does. And I wake up around six-thirty, just like always, before the alarm even goes off. Sometimes I'll wake up once in the middle of the night to use the restroom, but at my age, well, that's just par for the course, isn't it? \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say my sleep has changed. It's just... I wake up, and there's that blankness again. I just lie there in the morning, looking at the sunlight coming through the blinds, and I don't want to get up, but I'm not tired, either. I'm just... there. But the actual sleeping part, the falling asleep and staying asleep, that hasn't changed. It's just the waking up to the same empty house that's hard. But the sleep itself is fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you have less energy than usual or feel more tired, even after doing simple tasks like getting dressed or making a meal?\n\nPatient: Energy? Well, let me think about that. I wouldn't say I'm physically tired, no. I really don't think my energy has changed at all. I still get up and do my morning routine just like I always have. \n\nYou mentioned getting dressed... well, I still get dressed every single morning. I have this cedar-lined closet my husband built for us back in the seventies—it still smells wonderful if you just scratch the wood a little bit with your fingernail—and I go in there and pick out my clothes. Usually, it's just a pair of comfortable slacks, the ones with the elastic waist that I order from that catalog that comes in the mail, and a nice blouse. I have a whole row of blouses, mostly pastels, pinks and soft blues. I still iron them all on Sunday afternoons, just like I always do. Standing at the ironing board doesn't tire me out in the slightest. I can stand there for an hour, pressing the collars and the cuffs, making sure there are no wrinkles, because my mother always taught me that a lady never leaves her bedroom looking rumpled, even if she's not expecting a single visitor. So getting dressed is perfectly fine. I don't need to sit on the edge of the bed to catch my breath or anything like that. My body works just fine.\n\nAnd making meals, well, I told you about the toast and the soup earlier. I don't feel exhausted doing any of that. Opening the can, turning the dial on the stove... my hands and my arms have plenty of strength. I even swept the kitchen floor yesterday afternoon. I used the yellow broom, the one with the angled bristles that gets right under the baseboards perfectly. I swept the whole kitchen and the little hallway, right past the hall table where the paramedics were rushing past, and I didn't feel winded or tired at all. I gathered it all into the dustpan and threw it away, and I felt perfectly fine physically. \n\nSo my energy is just the same as it's always been. I've always been a doer, doctor. I keep my house tidy, I do my chores. It's not that I don't have the energy to do them. It's just that... well, when I'm standing there with the broom, or buttoning up my pastel blouse, I just don't feel anything inside. I'm not tired, I'm just hollow. The energy is there in my arms and my legs, and my body does what it's supposed to do, but it just feels so pointless. I could probably walk all the way down to the grocery store on Elm Street right now if I really needed to, I have the stamina for it, but there's just no reason to go. So no, I wouldn't say I'm more tired. I just don't care that I'm awake.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself withdrawing from people or not wanting to be around others, even people you care about, like your family or neighbors?\n\nPatient: Withdrawing? Oh, no, doctor, I wouldn't say that at all. I've always been a very sociable person, and that hasn't changed one bit. Just... well, just yesterday, Martha from next door—she lives in the green house with the lovely azalea bushes out front, though they're looking a little brown this year because of the dry spring we had—anyway, Martha came over and rang the bell. We have one of those old two-tone chimes, ding-dong, you know, not a harsh buzzer. My husband fixed the wiring on it back in '82 and it's worked perfectly ever since. \n\nWell, I didn't hesitate to open the door for her. I invited her right into the front parlor. She brought over a lemon pound cake, wrapped in that heavy-duty aluminum foil, and I made us a pot of tea. I even used the good china, the Royal Doulton with the little gold rim that we got for our silver anniversary. I didn't try to rush her out or anything. We sat there for a good hour, and she told me all about her grandson's graduation from the state college. I listened to every word, I really did. \n\nAnd my family, well, I certainly haven't been avoiding them. My youngest, David, he calls every single evening right at 7:15, just after the game shows end on television. I never let it go to the answering machine. I pick up on the second ring, just like I always have, and I ask him about his day, and how his children are doing in school. I don't cut the conversations short, either. We talk for a good twenty minutes, sometimes thirty if he's got a story about the dog. \n\nSo, no, I'm not hiding away or anything like that. If someone comes up the driveway, I don't pull the curtains and pretend I'm not home. I still went to my Thursday morning knitting circle at the community center this week, too. I sat in my usual chair, the second one from the window. I don't really feel much while I'm there, of course, just that same blank feeling I told you about earlier, like I'm watching everyone talk through a thick pane of glass. But I'm physically there, and I smile, and I pass the yarn when they ask for it. I don't mind the company at all. So I wouldn't say I'm withdrawing or not wanting to be around people. I'm still doing all my socializing and seeing my neighbors, just exactly like I always have.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty about things you've done or believing that you're not worth much since your husband's incident?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Oh, goodness, no. Let me think... no, I certainly don't feel guilty about anything. I mean, why would I? When... when the incident happened, when he collapsed, I did exactly what I was supposed to do. I didn't freeze up or do anything foolish. I went straight to the telephone. We still have the wall phone in the kitchen, you know, the one with the extra-long coiled cord so you can stir a pot on the stove while you're talking? It's a lovely harvest gold color, matches the old oven perfectly. Anyway, I picked it up and dialed 9-1-1 immediately. \n\nThe operator told me to make sure the front door was unlocked, so I walked right over to the foyer. We have a heavy deadbolt on that door—my husband installed it after we read an article about burglaries in the Sunday paper back in 1994—and you have to jiggle the latch just a tiny bit to get it to turn smoothly, but I got it open right away. I even turned on the porch light so the paramedics could see the house numbers clearly. The brass numbers, I polish them every spring. So, I did everything perfectly right. The paramedics even told me I was very helpful. There's just no reason in the world for me to feel guilty. \n\nAnd as for feeling like I'm not worth much... well, no, I wouldn't say that either. I know my own value, doctor. I've been a good wife, a good mother. I've kept a beautiful home for over fifty years. Just because this awful thing happened doesn't erase who I am or what I've built. I still manage everything just fine. Every Tuesday morning, I sit down at the dining room table—the mahogany one we bought at Sears Roebuck when we first got married, I still use a lace tablecloth on it to prevent scratches—and I get out my checkbook. The one with the little Thomas Kinkade paintings on the checks. I write out the checks for the electric bill, the water bill, the newspaper subscription, and I balance the ledger down to the very last penny. \n\nA person who isn't worth much wouldn't be able to keep a household running so efficiently, would they? I still make sure the trash cans are out by the curb on Wednesday nights, and I still remember to water the Boston fern in the sunroom. So, no, my self-worth is perfectly intact. I don't feel guilty, and I don't feel worthless. It's just... like I said before, it's just this heavy blankness. I know I did my best, and I know I'm still a capable woman, but knowing that doesn't make the house feel any less empty. But to answer your question, no, no guilt at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been moving more slowly than usual, or that it takes you longer to get things done, like buttoning your blouse or walking across the room?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Oh, heavens no, I wouldn't say that. I've always had a very brisk step, doctor. Even when I was a young woman, people used to tell me I walked like I had somewhere important to be, and that hasn't changed, not one bit. \n\nYou mentioned buttoning a blouse... well, just this morning, I put on my favorite soft blue one. It’s the one with the little pearl buttons down the front—not the cheap plastic ones, but real mother-of-pearl that catches the light so beautifully if you stand near the bedroom window. I buttoned it right up in no time at all. My fingers are just as nimble as they ever were. I don't fumble with the buttonholes, and I certainly don't take any longer to get dressed than the fifteen minutes I've allotted myself every morning since 1962. \n\nAnd walking across the room? No, no, I'm not shuffling or dragging my feet. We have this beautiful runner rug in the main hallway—it's an oriental pattern, lots of deep reds and navy blues, which we bought at an estate sale over in Westbridge about thirty years ago to protect the hardwood floors—and I walk down that hallway at the exact same pace I always have. I know precisely how long it takes me to get from the bedroom to the kitchen to put the kettle on. I walk right past the grandfather clock my husband used to wind every Sunday, and I don't take a second longer to cross the house than I used to. \n\nI even walked all the way down the driveway to the mailbox yesterday afternoon. It's a fairly long driveway, paved with asphalt that gets terribly hot in the summer, though it's quite pleasant right now, and I marched right down there, retrieved the mail, and walked right back up. Most of it was just catalogs and a flyer for the new supermarket, nothing important, but I didn't need to stop and rest or catch my breath. \n\nSo no, my body isn't slowing down. I can move just as quickly and efficiently as I ever did. It's just... well, like I told you earlier, even though my legs work perfectly fine and I can walk just as fast, it just feels like there's nowhere that really matters to walk to anymore. But my physical speed? Oh, that's perfectly normal. I haven't slowed down a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about harming yourself or not wanting to live anymore?\n\nPatient: Harming myself? Oh, my goodness, doctor, no. I could never do something like that. I've always believed life is a precious thing, even when it gets difficult. I certainly haven't had any thoughts about not wanting to be here. \n\nWhy, just this morning, I was sitting at the kitchen island—it has that lovely butcher block top that my husband oiled every single month without fail. He always used food-grade mineral oil, never olive oil, because olive oil can go rancid over time and ruin the wood, you know. I have one of those little plastic pill organizers, the ones with the days of the week printed in bold black letters on the lids. Mine is a cheerful bright pink color. My son David bought it for me at the pharmacy on Main Street about three years ago because I kept forgetting if I'd taken my calcium supplement. \n\nEvery single morning, I pop open the little lid for the day, and I take my calcium pill, a vitamin D, and a fish oil capsule, and I swallow them down with a small glass of orange juice. The pulp-free kind, of course, because I just can't stand the texture of pulp in my drinks. Now, if I didn't want to live, I certainly wouldn't be going through all that trouble every single day to keep my bones strong and my heart healthy, would I? \n\nI've never once thought about doing anything to hurt myself. It's a terrible thing to even consider. I know some people get to that dark place when something awful happens, but that's just not me. Even with this... this heavy, quiet feeling in the house, and that blankness I mentioned before, I still get up and I still take care of myself. I still make sure I'm eating my three meals, I still lock the doors at night, and I still look both ways before I cross the street to get to the community center. I'm going to keep right on living, just exactly like I'm supposed to. So no, doctor, absolutely no thoughts like that at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you felt unusually high, overly energetic, or extremely irritable, where you didn’t seem to need much sleep or your thoughts were racing?\n\nPatient: Unusually high or overly energetic? Oh, heavens, no. I'm not that sort of person at all, doctor. I've always been very even-keeled. My husband used to say I was as predictable as the sunrise, and he meant it as a compliment, of course. I don't get overly worked up or go flying around the house with too much energy, and I certainly don't have racing thoughts. My mind is usually very organized. \n\nAnd as for not needing much sleep... well, I've always valued a good night's rest. Every single night, for as long as we've lived in this house, I start getting ready for bed at exactly nine-thirty. I go into the master bathroom—the one with the peach-colored tiles we had put in back in 1978, they were very popular then, you know, though I suppose they look a bit dated now—and I wash my face with cold cream. The Pond's cold cream, the classic one with the light green lid. Then I brush my teeth and I'm in bed by ten o'clock sharp. I have a very specific mattress, a firm Sealy Posturepedic, because a soft mattress is just terrible for your lumbar spine, and I sleep right through until six-thirty in the morning. I've never been one of those people who can just stay up all night or run around on two hours of sleep. Goodness, I'd be absolutely exhausted! I simply must have my eight hours.\n\nAnd irritable? No, no, I wouldn't say so. I try to be pleasant to everyone, I really do. Even when the neighbor's boy throws the morning paper right into my azalea bushes instead of onto the front porch—which he's done three times this month, mind you, and those bushes are very delicate, especially the pink ones—I just put on my slippers and go out and retrieve it myself. I don't fly off the handle or get terribly irritated with him. He's just a boy on a bicycle, after all. It's just not in my nature to be snappy or irritable. \n\nSo, no, I've never had a time like that where I was just bursting with energy or my mind was going a mile a minute. I've always been very steady, very calm. Just... well, just a bit quieter lately, I suppose. But no periods of being unusually high or anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. That's the thing that's so hard to explain to my daughter, because she keeps asking me, \"Mom, are you depressed? Are you crying?\" And I tell her, no, I'm not crying. I haven't shed a single tear, which is strange for me, because I've always been a bit of a weeper, you know? A sad commercial on the television, or a sweet card from the grandchildren, and I'd be reaching for a tissue. But now? No. \n\nIt's not that heavy, weeping kind of sadness that you might expect. It's more like... well, like I was saying before, it's just this blankness. I don't wake up feeling like there's a dark cloud over my head, making me want to sob into my pillow. I wake up, and I look at the clock—it's usually around 6:15, my body just naturally wakes up then after all these years—and I just feel empty. Not sad, just... nothing. I go about my morning, I make my toast, I pour my decaf coffee, and I just sit there at the kitchen table, looking out the window, and I don't feel down, exactly. I just don't feel anything at all. So, no, I wouldn't say I feel sad or down most days. It's more of an absence of feeling, really, rather than a sad feeling.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you feel a depressed mood most days, even when there isn't a specific reason for it?\n\nPatient: Well, no, doctor, I... I feel like we might be going around in circles a bit here, because it's just like I was trying to explain a moment ago. It's not a depressed mood, not in the way you're asking. When I think of being depressed, I think of my sister, Mary—she passed away back in 2012, God rest her soul—but when she was going through her divorce in the late seventies, oh, she was depressed. She couldn't get out of bed, she cried all the time, she wore her bathrobe until three in the afternoon. That's a depressed mood to me. \n\nBut for me, right now, it's not that at all. I get up, I get dressed. I put on a nice blouse, I fix my hair, even if I'm not going anywhere but the front porch. I don't feel that heavy, sinking, tearful kind of sadness. I'm not sitting around weeping for no reason. It's just... well, it's that emptiness I mentioned. It's like my heart is just a hollow room right now. There's no sadness in it, but there's no happiness either. It's just quiet. Too quiet. So, no, I really wouldn't call it a depressed mood. I wouldn't use that word at all. I just feel completely blank.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in all or almost all activities, including those you used to enjoy, like tending to your garden or being with your family?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I... forgive me, but I have to ask if you're looking at the right page in your notes there. Because, well, unless my hearing aids are acting up again—and I just put fresh batteries in them this morning, the little size 312 ones, they always give me such a fuss to get into the little door with my arthritis—I could swear you just asked me this exact same thing. About the garden and the family. \n\nI mean, I really don't know what else I can tell you that I haven't already said just a moment ago. Yes, I've lost interest. Just like I was explaining with the porch and the wicker chair and my grandchildren. I haven't even gone out to check on my tomato plants, and usually, I'm out there with my sun hat and my little green watering can every single morning before the sun gets too hot. But now, I just look out the kitchen window at them and I don't feel the slightest urge to go out there. \n\nIt's all just exactly what I said before. That blankness. I really don't have anything new to add about it, doctor. I don't mean to be difficult, I really don't, but we seem to be going over the exact same ground here, and I've already told you everything about how I'm just not enjoying my family or my yard the way I used to. Is there... is there some other part of it you're trying to figure out? Because I just don't have anything else to say about the gardening.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the emptiness and loss of interest in your usual activities?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... it's been about two weeks now. Yes, exactly two weeks this past Tuesday. I remember because Tuesday is usually the day the trash gets picked up, and I was just standing there at the kitchen counter, trying to decide if I should take the bin out myself or wait for my neighbor, Mr. Henderson, to offer—he's such a nice man, always looks out for me—when it happened. \n\nThat was the day my husband... well, that was the day he collapsed. Right there in our kitchen. We've had that same linoleum floor since 1998, a sort of pale yellow pattern, and he just went down on it so suddenly. One minute he was pouring himself a glass of orange juice, and the next, he was just on the floor. I called 911, and the paramedics came. It felt like it took them hours, but I know it was only a few minutes. They rushed in with all their equipment, pushing past my little hall table and knocking over a picture frame—not that I cared about the frame, of course, it was just a picture of the dog we had in the nineties—and they worked on him right there on the floor. \n\nThey were shouting things, numbers and medical terms I didn't understand, and the radio on their shoulders was crackling, and I just stood there, backed into the corner by the refrigerator, watching them pump his chest. It was so loud, doctor, and so chaotic. And ever since that morning, ever since they finally got him on the stretcher and took him out the front door... that's when this emptiness started. Two weeks of just... nothing. Just sitting in that same kitchen, looking at the spot on the floor, and feeling completely blank. So, yes, it's been about two weeks since all of this started.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight recently?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I really don't think so, doctor. I mean, my weight has always been pretty steady, ever since I had my youngest, David, back in '68. I've always hovered right around the same mark on the scale. We have this old scale in the bathroom, one of the ones with the little dial that spins around, not those new digital things with the red numbers. It belonged to my mother, actually, and it's got a little rust spot near the left footpad, but it still works just fine. And I haven't noticed the dial moving any differently than it usually does. \n\nAs for my appetite... well, I suppose I'm eating about the same as I always have. I'm not really paying much attention to it, to be honest. I still make my toast in the morning—two slices of wheat bread, toasted just enough to be warm but not too crunchy, with a little scrape of butter. And I still have my cup of tea. But I'm just eating because it's time to eat, you know? It's like I'm going through the motions. \n\nI'll sit down at the kitchen table—right near where he fell, but I try not to look at that spot—and I'll chew my toast, and I don't really taste it. It's just cardboard in my mouth. But I finish it, because that's what you do at breakfast time. And for dinner, I might heat up a can of soup. Chicken noodle, usually, the kind with the little star-shaped pasta. My husband always liked the chunky kind, but I prefer the stars. But even the soup, it's just... it's just warm liquid. I'm not hungry for it, but I'm not actively avoiding it either. I just eat it because the clock says it's six o'clock. So, no, I wouldn't say my appetite has changed, not really. I'm still eating my regular meals. It's just that the food doesn't mean anything anymore. It's just another blank thing in a blank day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately, like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up earlier than usual and not being able to go back to sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, sleep... sleep is about the same, I suppose. I mean, I'm not really having any new trouble with it. I've always been a bit of a light sleeper, you know? Ever since I was a girl, my mother used to say a mouse sneezing three houses down could wake me up. And then when my husband and I bought this house back in '72, we got the bedroom right over the street, and for years I'd wake up every time the milkman came by. \n\nSo I still go to bed around the same time, right after the ten o'clock news finishes. I always watch the local news, channel 4, because I like the weatherman, the one with the bright ties. And then I go upstairs, and I do my little routine—wash my face with that cold cream I've used for fifty years, brush my teeth, put on my nightgown. The blue cotton one with the little embroidered flowers around the collar. \n\nAnd I get into bed, and... well, I fall asleep eventually. I might lie there for a bit, staring at the ceiling fan—we put that fan in during the heatwave of '88, and it's got this one blade that wobbles just a tiny bit if you put it on the highest speed—but I don't think it takes me any longer to drift off than it usually does. And I wake up around six-thirty, just like always, before the alarm even goes off. Sometimes I'll wake up once in the middle of the night to use the restroom, but at my age, well, that's just par for the course, isn't it? \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say my sleep has changed. It's just... I wake up, and there's that blankness again. I just lie there in the morning, looking at the sunlight coming through the blinds, and I don't want to get up, but I'm not tired, either. I'm just... there. But the actual sleeping part, the falling asleep and staying asleep, that hasn't changed. It's just the waking up to the same empty house that's hard. But the sleep itself is fine.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you have less energy than usual or feel more tired, even after doing simple tasks like getting dressed or making a meal?\n\nPatient: Energy? Well, let me think about that. I wouldn't say I'm physically tired, no. I really don't think my energy has changed at all. I still get up and do my morning routine just like I always have. \n\nYou mentioned getting dressed... well, I still get dressed every single morning. I have this cedar-lined closet my husband built for us back in the seventies—it still smells wonderful if you just scratch the wood a little bit with your fingernail—and I go in there and pick out my clothes. Usually, it's just a pair of comfortable slacks, the ones with the elastic waist that I order from that catalog that comes in the mail, and a nice blouse. I have a whole row of blouses, mostly pastels, pinks and soft blues. I still iron them all on Sunday afternoons, just like I always do. Standing at the ironing board doesn't tire me out in the slightest. I can stand there for an hour, pressing the collars and the cuffs, making sure there are no wrinkles, because my mother always taught me that a lady never leaves her bedroom looking rumpled, even if she's not expecting a single visitor. So getting dressed is perfectly fine. I don't need to sit on the edge of the bed to catch my breath or anything like that. My body works just fine.\n\nAnd making meals, well, I told you about the toast and the soup earlier. I don't feel exhausted doing any of that. Opening the can, turning the dial on the stove... my hands and my arms have plenty of strength. I even swept the kitchen floor yesterday afternoon. I used the yellow broom, the one with the angled bristles that gets right under the baseboards perfectly. I swept the whole kitchen and the little hallway, right past the hall table where the paramedics were rushing past, and I didn't feel winded or tired at all. I gathered it all into the dustpan and threw it away, and I felt perfectly fine physically. \n\nSo my energy is just the same as it's always been. I've always been a doer, doctor. I keep my house tidy, I do my chores. It's not that I don't have the energy to do them. It's just that... well, when I'm standing there with the broom, or buttoning up my pastel blouse, I just don't feel anything inside. I'm not tired, I'm just hollow. The energy is there in my arms and my legs, and my body does what it's supposed to do, but it just feels so pointless. I could probably walk all the way down to the grocery store on Elm Street right now if I really needed to, I have the stamina for it, but there's just no reason to go. So no, I wouldn't say I'm more tired. I just don't care that I'm awake.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself withdrawing from people or not wanting to be around others, even people you care about, like your family or neighbors?\n\nPatient: Withdrawing? Oh, no, doctor, I wouldn't say that at all. I've always been a very sociable person, and that hasn't changed one bit. Just... well, just yesterday, Martha from next door—she lives in the green house with the lovely azalea bushes out front, though they're looking a little brown this year because of the dry spring we had—anyway, Martha came over and rang the bell. We have one of those old two-tone chimes, ding-dong, you know, not a harsh buzzer. My husband fixed the wiring on it back in '82 and it's worked perfectly ever since. \n\nWell, I didn't hesitate to open the door for her. I invited her right into the front parlor. She brought over a lemon pound cake, wrapped in that heavy-duty aluminum foil, and I made us a pot of tea. I even used the good china, the Royal Doulton with the little gold rim that we got for our silver anniversary. I didn't try to rush her out or anything. We sat there for a good hour, and she told me all about her grandson's graduation from the state college. I listened to every word, I really did. \n\nAnd my family, well, I certainly haven't been avoiding them. My youngest, David, he calls every single evening right at 7:15, just after the game shows end on television. I never let it go to the answering machine. I pick up on the second ring, just like I always have, and I ask him about his day, and how his children are doing in school. I don't cut the conversations short, either. We talk for a good twenty minutes, sometimes thirty if he's got a story about the dog. \n\nSo, no, I'm not hiding away or anything like that. If someone comes up the driveway, I don't pull the curtains and pretend I'm not home. I still went to my Thursday morning knitting circle at the community center this week, too. I sat in my usual chair, the second one from the window. I don't really feel much while I'm there, of course, just that same blank feeling I told you about earlier, like I'm watching everyone talk through a thick pane of glass. But I'm physically there, and I smile, and I pass the yarn when they ask for it. I don't mind the company at all. So I wouldn't say I'm withdrawing or not wanting to be around people. I'm still doing all my socializing and seeing my neighbors, just exactly like I always have.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling guilty about things you've done or believing that you're not worth much since your husband's incident?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Oh, goodness, no. Let me think... no, I certainly don't feel guilty about anything. I mean, why would I? When... when the incident happened, when he collapsed, I did exactly what I was supposed to do. I didn't freeze up or do anything foolish. I went straight to the telephone. We still have the wall phone in the kitchen, you know, the one with the extra-long coiled cord so you can stir a pot on the stove while you're talking? It's a lovely harvest gold color, matches the old oven perfectly. Anyway, I picked it up and dialed 9-1-1 immediately. \n\nThe operator told me to make sure the front door was unlocked, so I walked right over to the foyer. We have a heavy deadbolt on that door—my husband installed it after we read an article about burglaries in the Sunday paper back in 1994—and you have to jiggle the latch just a tiny bit to get it to turn smoothly, but I got it open right away. I even turned on the porch light so the paramedics could see the house numbers clearly. The brass numbers, I polish them every spring. So, I did everything perfectly right. The paramedics even told me I was very helpful. There's just no reason in the world for me to feel guilty. \n\nAnd as for feeling like I'm not worth much... well, no, I wouldn't say that either. I know my own value, doctor. I've been a good wife, a good mother. I've kept a beautiful home for over fifty years. Just because this awful thing happened doesn't erase who I am or what I've built. I still manage everything just fine. Every Tuesday morning, I sit down at the dining room table—the mahogany one we bought at Sears Roebuck when we first got married, I still use a lace tablecloth on it to prevent scratches—and I get out my checkbook. The one with the little Thomas Kinkade paintings on the checks. I write out the checks for the electric bill, the water bill, the newspaper subscription, and I balance the ledger down to the very last penny. \n\nA person who isn't worth much wouldn't be able to keep a household running so efficiently, would they? I still make sure the trash cans are out by the curb on Wednesday nights, and I still remember to water the Boston fern in the sunroom. So, no, my self-worth is perfectly intact. I don't feel guilty, and I don't feel worthless. It's just... like I said before, it's just this heavy blankness. I know I did my best, and I know I'm still a capable woman, but knowing that doesn't make the house feel any less empty. But to answer your question, no, no guilt at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been moving more slowly than usual, or that it takes you longer to get things done, like buttoning your blouse or walking across the room?\n\nPatient: Moving slowly? Oh, heavens no, I wouldn't say that. I've always had a very brisk step, doctor. Even when I was a young woman, people used to tell me I walked like I had somewhere important to be, and that hasn't changed, not one bit. \n\nYou mentioned buttoning a blouse... well, just this morning, I put on my favorite soft blue one. It’s the one with the little pearl buttons down the front—not the cheap plastic ones, but real mother-of-pearl that catches the light so beautifully if you stand near the bedroom window. I buttoned it right up in no time at all. My fingers are just as nimble as they ever were. I don't fumble with the buttonholes, and I certainly don't take any longer to get dressed than the fifteen minutes I've allotted myself every morning since 1962. \n\nAnd walking across the room? No, no, I'm not shuffling or dragging my feet. We have this beautiful runner rug in the main hallway—it's an oriental pattern, lots of deep reds and navy blues, which we bought at an estate sale over in Westbridge about thirty years ago to protect the hardwood floors—and I walk down that hallway at the exact same pace I always have. I know precisely how long it takes me to get from the bedroom to the kitchen to put the kettle on. I walk right past the grandfather clock my husband used to wind every Sunday, and I don't take a second longer to cross the house than I used to. \n\nI even walked all the way down the driveway to the mailbox yesterday afternoon. It's a fairly long driveway, paved with asphalt that gets terribly hot in the summer, though it's quite pleasant right now, and I marched right down there, retrieved the mail, and walked right back up. Most of it was just catalogs and a flyer for the new supermarket, nothing important, but I didn't need to stop and rest or catch my breath. \n\nSo no, my body isn't slowing down. I can move just as quickly and efficiently as I ever did. It's just... well, like I told you earlier, even though my legs work perfectly fine and I can walk just as fast, it just feels like there's nowhere that really matters to walk to anymore. But my physical speed? Oh, that's perfectly normal. I haven't slowed down a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about harming yourself or not wanting to live anymore?\n\nPatient: Harming myself? Oh, my goodness, doctor, no. I could never do something like that. I've always believed life is a precious thing, even when it gets difficult. I certainly haven't had any thoughts about not wanting to be here. \n\nWhy, just this morning, I was sitting at the kitchen island—it has that lovely butcher block top that my husband oiled every single month without fail. He always used food-grade mineral oil, never olive oil, because olive oil can go rancid over time and ruin the wood, you know. Anyway, I was sitting right there on the wooden stool, taking my daily vitamins. I have one of those little plastic pill organizers, the ones with the days of the week printed in bold black letters on the lids. Mine is a cheerful bright pink color. My son David bought it for me at the pharmacy on Main Street about three years ago because I kept forgetting if I'd taken my calcium supplement. \n\nEvery single morning, I pop open the little lid for the day, and I take my calcium pill, a vitamin D, and a fish oil capsule, and I swallow them down with a small glass of orange juice. The pulp-free kind, of course, because I just can't stand the texture of pulp in my drinks. Now, if I didn't want to live, I certainly wouldn't be going through all that trouble every single day to keep my bones strong and my heart healthy, would I? \n\nI've never once thought about doing anything to hurt myself. It's a terrible thing to even consider. I know some people get to that dark place when something awful happens, but that's just not me. Even with this... this heavy, quiet feeling in the house, and that blankness I mentioned before, I still get up and I still take care of myself. I still make sure I'm eating my three meals, I still lock the doors at night, and I still look both ways before I cross the street to get to the community center. I'm going to keep right on living, just exactly like I'm supposed to. So no, doctor, absolutely no thoughts like that at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you felt unusually high, overly energetic, or extremely irritable, where you didn’t seem to need much sleep or your thoughts were racing?\n\nPatient: Unusually high or overly energetic? Oh, heavens, no. I'm not that sort of person at all, doctor. I've always been very even-keeled. My husband used to say I was as predictable as the sunrise, and he meant it as a compliment, of course. I don't get overly worked up or go flying around the house with too much energy, and I certainly don't have racing thoughts. My mind is usually very organized. \n\nAnd as for not needing much sleep... well, I've always valued a good night's rest. Every single night, for as long as we've lived in this house, I start getting ready for bed at exactly nine-thirty. I go into the master bathroom—the one with the peach-colored tiles we had put in back in 1978, they were very popular then, you know, though I suppose they look a bit dated now—and I wash my face with cold cream. The Pond's cold cream, the classic one with the light green lid. Then I brush my teeth and I'm in bed by ten o'clock sharp. I have a very specific mattress, a firm Sealy Posturepedic, because a soft mattress is just terrible for your lumbar spine, and I sleep right through until six-thirty in the morning. I've never been one of those people who can just stay up all night or run around on two hours of sleep. Goodness, I'd be absolutely exhausted! I simply must have my eight hours.\n\nAnd irritable? No, no, I wouldn't say so. I try to be pleasant to everyone, I really do. Even when the neighbor's boy throws the morning paper right into my azalea bushes instead of onto the front porch—which he's done three times this month, mind you, and those bushes are very delicate, especially the pink ones—I just put on my slippers and go out and retrieve it myself. I don't fly off the handle or get terribly irritated with him. He's just a boy on a bicycle, after all. It's just not in my nature to be snappy or irritable. \n\nSo, no, I've never had a time like that where I was just bursting with energy or my mind was going a mile a minute. I've always been very steady, very calm. Just... well, just a bit quieter lately, I suppose. But no periods of being unusually high or anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, it's not exactly that I don't want to do things, doctor. It's a bit harder to explain than just losing interest. You mentioned the garden... I have a very lovely garden out in the backyard. My husband and I put the raised beds in ourselves back in 1985. We have these gorgeous hydrangea bushes along the back fence—the big mophead kind, you know, that turn a brilliant, vivid blue if you remember to put just the right amount of aluminum sulfate in the soil around the roots every spring. I used to spend hours out there. I'd wear my wide-brimmed straw hat and just feel the warmth of the afternoon sun on my shoulders, and it used to bring me such a deep, wonderful sense of peace. Just absolute contentment. 

But lately... well, honestly, that's the main reason I finally decided I needed to come and talk to you. Because even when the weather is perfect, and the sun is shining right down on my garden, illuminating those blue petals just the way I always loved, I just... I feel completely flat inside. There's no joy in it. 

And it's the exact same way with my family. My children have been so very good to me. My son David and my daughter Susan, they come over and they sit with me in the living room. They usually sit right on the floral sofa—the one we had completely reupholstered about five years ago because the original fabric was getting terribly threadbare along the armrests. They'll sit right there, and they'll hold my hands, and they'll talk to me about their days, trying so hard to be there for me. 

But even when my children sit with me, trying to bring me a little bit of comfort... it's just nothing. It feels like the part of me that could be glad they're visiting, or the part that used to feel comforted by their company, it just shut off entirely. Like a lamp someone just unplugged from the wall. I look at them, and I know in my head that they are my wonderful children, but I can't feel the warmth of it anymore. Just that terrible, quiet flatness, no matter what I do.

So, no, I wouldn't say I feel sad or down most days. It's more of an absence of feeling, really, rather than a sad feeling.\n\nDoctor: Would you say you feel a depressed mood most days, even when there isn't a specific reason for it?\n\nPatient: Well, no, doctor, I... When I think of being depressed, I think of my sister, Mary—she passed away back in 2012, God rest her soul—but when she was going through her divorce in the late seventies, oh, she was depressed. She couldn't get out of bed, she cried all the time, she wore her bathrobe until three in the afternoon. That's a depressed mood to me. \n\nBut for me, right now, it's not that at all. Because I just don't have anything else to say about the gardening.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the emptiness and loss of interest in your usual activities?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think... it's been about two weeks now. Yes, exactly two weeks this past Tuesday. I remember because Tuesday is usually the day the trash gets picked up, and I was just standing there at the kitchen counter, trying to decide if I should take the bin out myself or wait for my neighbor, Mr. Henderson, to offer—he's such a nice man, always looks out for me—when it happened. \n\nThat was the day my husband... well, that was the day he collapsed. Right there in our kitchen. We've had that same linoleum floor since 1998, a sort of pale yellow pattern, and he just went down on it so suddenly. One minute he was pouring himself a glass of orange juice, and the next, he was just on the floor. I called 911, and the paramedics came. It felt like it took them hours, but I know it was only a few minutes. They rushed in with all their equipment, pushing past my little hall table and knocking over a picture frame—not that I cared about the frame, of course, it was just a picture of the dog we had in the nineties—and they worked on him right there on the floor. \n\nThey were shouting things, numbers and medical terms I didn't understand, and the radio on their shoulders was crackling, and I just stood there, backed into the corner by the refrigerator, watching them pump his chest. It was so loud, doctor, and so chaotic. And ever since that morning, ever since they finally got him on the stretcher and took him out the front door... that's when this emptiness started. We have this old scale in the bathroom, one of the ones with the little dial that spins around, not those new digital things with the red numbers. It belonged to my mother, actually, and it's got a little rust spot near the left footpad, but it still works just fine. I'm not really paying much attention to it, to be honest. It's like I'm going through the motions. \n\nI'll sit down at the kitchen table—right near where he fell, but I try not to look at that spot—and I'll chew my toast, and I don't really taste it. It's just cardboard in my mouth. But I finish it, because that's what you do at breakfast time. And for dinner, I might heat up a can of soup. Chicken noodle, usually, the kind with the little star-shaped pasta. My husband always liked the chunky kind, but I prefer the stars. But even the soup, it's just... it's just warm liquid. I'm not hungry for it, but I'm not actively avoiding it either. I just eat it because the clock says it's six o'clock. So, no, I wouldn't say my appetite has changed, not really. I'm still eating my regular meals. It's just that the food doesn't mean anything anymore. It's just another blank thing in a blank day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep lately, like trouble falling asleep, waking up during the night, or waking up earlier than usual and not being able to go back to sleep?\n\nPatient: Well, sleep... sleep is about the same, I suppose. I mean, I'm not really having any new trouble with it. I've always been a bit of a light sleeper, you know? Ever since I was a girl, my mother used to say a mouse sneezing three houses down could wake me up. And then when my husband and I bought this house back in '72, we got the bedroom right over the street, and for years I'd wake up every time the milkman came by. \n\nSo I still go to bed around the same time, right after the ten o'clock news finishes. I always watch the local news, channel 4, because I like the weatherman, the one with the bright ties. The blue cotton one with the little embroidered flowers around the collar. \n\nAnd I get into bed, and... well, I fall asleep eventually.
